# Pitch

## Context & Idea

### Goal

Increase ARPU 14d for non-referral users in UG App by optimizing the first session flow after tour. Currently, 50% of first-session revenue comes from the tour paywall and 50% from post-tour actions — optimizing the post-PW1 flow effectively doubles the conversion surface.

### Foundation

13 experiments have systematically narrowed the solution space. We now know with high confidence:

The tour is already optimal ( 5 new tour steps , 5 pre-paywall iterations — all failed)

Price reduction / discounts don't work ( Weekly plan $1.99 : CR +30% but ARPU -36%; Discount after PW1 : all metrics negative)

Showing full value before paywall kills trial→charge rate ( Duolingo warm-up screens : trial +22%, charge -17%)

Forcing actions destroys best segments ( Minimum 3 songs selection : 65% stopped at minimum)

Showing song lists ≠ showing the product ( Song search moved to tour start : +33-40% saw songs, 0 effect on conversion)

### Idea

After PW1 dismiss, redirect the user into a guided "play your first song" flow that follows the 8-step Job Map: find a song → see the tab → understand the structure → play one section → feel the result. The flow presents an Official Tab with a contextual paywall (PW2) before releasing to a free UGC version if skipped.

## Confidence research

There were a few types of research we conducted:

What is to play song? — Definition of "play a song" = play one section (chorus) at a slow tempo without stopping.

[macro]
Best practice research

Blinkist: honest trial timeline → +23% trial, +4% trial-to-paid, -55% complaints

FitnessAI: video before PW → +50% PW views, 2x install-to-trial

Lose It!: configure features → "keep what you set up" = primary monetization

Headspace: progressive locking for free users

Greg (plant app): showing PW to all in onboarding = 5x trial rate

[/macro]

[macro]
Research previous tour experiments to gather metrics and derive segments

Fact Source Implication for Stage 2

50% of trial users never open a tab Exp. Post-PW trial reminder Task #1: get them to the tab and make them play

40% of trial users cancel within the first hour Exp. Post-PW trial reminder Value must be immediate after purchase

75% complete the chorus in warm-up practice Exp. Warm-up chord practice Practice mode by sections works

Barre = abandon trigger ("I just won't learn it") JTBD interviews (Sam, Dave) Simplify / replace barres = Pro gate

CR+30% ARPU-36% on weekly plan Exp. Weekly plan $1.99 Conversion without ARPU = trap. Guardrail: plan mix

Trial+22%, charge-17% with full value before PW Exp. Duolingo warm-up Give partial value (1 section), not full

Banner CTR 1.9% vs 25% control after value demo Exp. Warm-up banner After value → contextual PW, not banner

"10-15 min to play, don't want to spend time on preparation" JTBD interview (Ray) Minimum steps to start playing

All users learn by sections, not the whole song JTBD (Sam, Dave, Zain — 9 interviews) "Play" = chorus at slow tempo

### What works (proven by 5 experiments)

Experiment Result What it proved

"Achieve" multiselect question SUCCESS Android CR +8.5-11.5% (sig), iOS D1 ret +4.5% (sig). Only confirmed win.

"What's holding you back?" question NEUTRAL No harm. 12% "want to try" = Conv 6.6%, ARPU $1.68 — best non-purchased segment.

"Do you know which song?" question NEUTRAL No harm. 64% know their song. But conv identical across groups.

Warm-up chord practice (early data) NEUTRAL 75% iOS / 72% Android complete. But post-WU banner CTR 1.9% vs 25% control.

[/macro]

## "What's holding you back?" (after PW1 dismiss)

Answer Share Conv to Charge ARPU

It's too expensive 41% 4.4% $0.95

I'm not interested 31% 1.4% $0.36

I want to try before I commit 12% 6.6% $1.68

I need more time to decide 8% 5.3% $1.35

Key insight: "Want to try" (12%) + "Need time" (8%) = 20% of users explicitly asking for value demo. These are the highest-converting non-purchased segments.

### Core Job: Play a song on guitar using tabs.

Step Job (step) Job Statement (JTBD format) Desired Outcomes Our Solution Evidence

1 Understand what's in front of me When I open a tab, I want to understand within 3 seconds — is this the right song, version, level — to decide whether to start or keep looking, without reading fine print. • Time to assess quality → min
• Probability of "wrong tab" → min
• Anxiety of "too difficult" → min • Official Tab badge "Most accurate"
• UGC: rating + top comment at the top
• Difficulty level (from tour) JTBD rank #1: trust accuracy
Chris: Official → I play right away
Ray: rating like IMDB

2 Customize for myself When I'm ready to play, I want to quickly adjust the tab to my instrument and level — so I don't have to stop later, without complex settings. • Steps to start playing → min
• Risk that barre kills motivation → min
• Tab-guitar mismatch → min • Transpose (exists)
• Capo suggestion
• Auto-suggest from tour (instrument, level) Sam: "won't learn with barre"
Dave: "barres really frustrating"
JTBD hyp #19: Replace barres

3 Understand the structure Before playing, I want to see what parts the song consists of and how many chords — to understand the scope and where to start, without the feeling of "endless wall of text". • Feeling of "too much" → min
• Understanding "where to start" → max
• Need to scroll → min • Song structure map (intro/verse/chorus)
• "Start here" highlight (chorus)
• "This song uses 4 chords" Dave: "learn a section, first 8 chords"
JTBD #6: step-by-step
JTBD #9: see all chords at once

4 Play a section When I start playing, I want to play one short part (chorus, 4-8 chords) at a slow tempo — to feel "I can do it", without having to play the whole thing. • Probability of success moment → max
• Stops due to unfamiliar chords → min
• Switching UG↔YouTube → min • Practice mode: chorus, step-by-step
• Visual feedback (no mic)
• Adapt by level from tour
• Repeat / slow down Warm-up: 75% complete chorus
Exp #6: partial value OK, full = kills urgency
Sam/Zain: learn in small pieces

5 Check myself While playing, I want to understand if it sounds right — so I don't memorize mistakes, without fully depending on my own ear. • Uncertainty "is it correct" → min
• Sense of progress → max
• Dependence on YouTube → min • Visual chord highlight (current one highlighted)
• Chord diagram on tap
• Strumming pattern display JTBD rank #3: real-time feedback
Zain: "can't give feedback"
Chris: "auto-grade"

6 Overcome a roadblock When I get stuck on a difficult chord transition, I want help specifically for that spot — so I don't abandon the song, without going to YouTube. • Abandon due to 1 difficult spot → min
• Time to find a solution → min
• Feeling "I can work around it" → max • Transpose (free)
• Loop / repeat section (free)
• Alternative chord suggestion Sam: "2 barres far apart → very slow"
Dave: transpose to remove barre
JTBD #7: optimal fingering

7 Feel the result After playing, I want to see that my efforts were worthwhile — to want to come back tomorrow, without the feeling of "I was fumbling around and achieved nothing". • Achievement feeling → max
• Risk "I'll never come back" → min
• Emotional reward → max • "Started" badge (>60 sec)
• "You played chorus of [song]"
• Micro-celebration JTBD hyp #12: "You've made it!" at 60s
40% cancel trial in 1st hour
Sam: "I can play what I couldn't"

8 Come back tomorrow When I close the app, I want to easily come back to the song tomorrow — to continue, without searching for the tab again. • Return friction → min
• Forgetting → min
• Continuity feeling → max • "Your songs" on Explore
• D1 push: "Continue [song]"
• "Next song" suggestion JTBD #43: Continue last song
JTBD #0: remember song
Jatan: list of played songs

## Hypothesis

If we guide the user to play their first song after tour install, then we will increase ARPU 14d by 10–20% without a drop in Retention 7d/14d, because more users will run into our premium content (and the paywall on it)

# Description of the Solution & Mockups

## Solution

Launch for segments

never had a trial / never had access

new users (with tour install)

### Step-by-step

How tour questions affect recommendations

Three tour questions act as filters for the song recommendation screen:

Level (Q3): Users who select "Absolute beginner" or "Three-chord strummer" receive recommendations from the Easy difficulty pool. All other levels (Campfire guitarist, Acoustic master, Rockstar) receive Medium difficulty recommendations.

Genres (Q10): Selected genres filter the recommendation pool.

Song picks (Q14 "Choose some songs you like"): Selected songs influence artist/genre weighting in recommendations.

The system shows 3 recommended songs based on these filters.

[macro]
Iteration 1
  Experiment flow (post-PW1)

Step 1: Song selection — "What do you want to play?"

Screen shows a search bar and 3 personalized recommendations (based on tour filters above)— all of them Official versions.

No "Continue" button — the user must tap a song to proceed.

Search results prioritize paid (Official/Pro) versions first, free (UGC) versions last.

We show only the top version of each song (no version list / no version selection screen). One song = one result row.

Recommendations are filtered by the genre(s) and level the user selected during the tour.

Referral song: If the user arrived via a referral link tied to a specific song, that song is included in the recommended songs list on this step. The referral song appears among the recommendations so the user can immediately find and play the song they were invited to try.

If the user selected Absolute beginner or Three-chord strummer : we show songs tagged Easy , filtered by their genre (e.g. one genre selected in the tour).

For all other levels ( Campfire guitarist , Acoustic master , Rockstar ): we show songs filtered by both the user's genre and their level tag.

Step 2: Format choice — "How do you want to play this song?"

User chooses between Chords ("Strum & sing") and Tabs ("Play the real riff") with visual previews of each format.

Note: Song cards in search show difficulty level — data comes from the backend.

Step 3: Official Tab auto-play

User lands on the Official (Pro) page based on previous question we show chosen type of song (chords or tabs).

Play starts automatically — the tab begins scrolling with sound immediately upon screen load.

After ~5 seconds: bottom sheet appears — "You're hearing the official version of tab" with value props (Full band sound, Beginner friendly, Note-by-note accurate).

The user can listen/play along for 15 seconds total.

Note: Timings (5s splash, 15s before paywall) are preliminary and will be refined during testing.

Step 4: Contextual paywall (PW2)

After 15 seconds, a contextual paywall appears: "Sound like [Artist] does" — referencing the song the user just heard.

Shows the artist card + Official tab badge.

CTA: "Try 7-days free, then $39.99/year. Cancel anytime."

Dismiss option: "I'm not that serious about it" — leads to the free UGC version of the same song.

Note: Secondary CTA "I'm not that serious about it" is chosen as a messaging contrast — "I'm not a serious player" vs primary CTA "Sound like [Artist] does".

Step 5a: Purchase → Pro tab

If user taps "Try for free" → Apple Pay confirmation → user stays on the Official (Pro) tab with full access and playback controls.

Step 5b: Dismiss → Free UGC tab

If user taps dismiss → redirected to the free (UGC) version of the same tab.

Since Step 1 already surfaces only the top-rated version of each song, the UGC tab the user lands on is the highest-rated community version available — no additional filtering is needed.

From this point, the user follows the standard current flow. Any Pro feature taps trigger the existing hard paywall (PW2 — "Choose your plan" with pricing).

Edge cases

No Official tab for the selected song: User lands on the community (UGC) tab. Paywall is NOT shown. User exits the flow into Explore.

User chose Chords on Step 2: Step 3 shows the Official tab in chord format (not tabs). The rest of the flow remains unchanged.

User has already used a trial: Excluded from the experiment. The flow is only shown to users who have NOT had a trial before.

Paywall behavior (Step 4): Play stops. Autoscroll stops. The paywall is shown in silence, with no background movement.

After paywall dismiss (Step 5b): User lands on the UGC version of the tab. Standard flow from here. Pro features trigger the regular hard paywall on features (same as control). After exiting the UGC tab — user goes to Explore.

PW1 discount screen: Shown once, only after the tour. Does NOT repeat in subsequent sessions.

[/macro]

[macro]
true Iteration 2 — Ceiling measurement
Goal: separate mechanics effect (autoplay / splash / timer-based paywall) from the effect of showing the Official Tab to new users at all. If ARPU is OK here, mechanics killed iteration 1. If ARPU is also poor, the problem is showing the tab as a first experience — and we stop building on this flow.

Decision source: direction agreed in Slack thread on 2026-04-17.

### Step-by-step

Steps 1–2: Song selection + Format choice

Identical to iteration 1 (kept as-is — these steps converted at 95–99% and are not the bottleneck).

Step 3: Official Tab with immediate fullscreen paywall

User lands on the Official (Pro) tab in chosen format (chords or tabs).

The tab is visible for a brief moment (on load, ~200–500 ms — native screen render time).

Fullscreen PW2 appears immediately on screen load — triggered by the Tab View event with from_tour=1 parameter. Not by a timer, not by a user action.

The paywall is the current production PW2 — same as shown in control when a non-Pro user taps a Pro feature. Same copy, same price, same dismiss behavior.

No autoplay of backing track. No autoscroll of the tab carriage. No splash overlay at 5s. No 15s timer before paywall. No access to Transpose / Loop / Play controls before the paywall (the user physically cannot reach them — the paywall is already on screen).

User decides based on the promise of Official Tab, not after consuming 15 seconds of it.

Step 4a: Purchase → Pro tab

User taps "Try for free" → Apple Pay → stays on Official (Pro) tab with full access.

Step 4b: Paywall dismiss → exit experiment

User closes paywall → follows the standard post-paywall flow (Explore or current production destination).

### What changed vs Iteration 1

In Iteration 1, the paywall opened on a timer — 15 seconds after the user landed on the Official Tab, preceded by a splash overlay at 5 seconds. The logic was "let the user hear/see the tab, then sell it." In Iteration 2 the paywall opens on the screen-load event, with no delay and no preceding interaction. Everything that could have masked the "tab as first experience" effect in Iteration 1 is removed.

### Edge cases

No Official tab for the selected song: we ask "Chords or Tabs?" and show the UGC tab in chosen format. Paywall is NOT shown. User exits the experiment.

User chose Chords on Step 2: Step 3 shows the Official tab in chord format. Paywall behavior unchanged.

User has already used a trial: excluded from the experiment.

PW1 discount screen: shown once, only after the tour. Does not repeat.

### What this tells us

If ARPU 14d ≥ control → Iteration 1 failed because of mechanics (value satiation from autoplay, bad timing, hostile splash). We can re-introduce elements one at a time in future iterations.

If ARPU 14d < control → showing Official Tab to new users is the wrong first experience. We stop building on this flow and move to pre-tab solutions: more tour questions before content ("looking for the perfect version for you" format), or a different first-session format for young / short-attention segments (current UX assumes pre-TikTok attention spans).

[/macro]

[macro]
Iteration 3
Goal: relaunch vB on a larger sample size prove the v2 ARPU lift at significance, try to catch droppers at vC.

A — Control. After PW1 the flow ends; user lands on Explore

B — Guided first song. After PW1 and instant offer, instead of ending, the user is taken into a first-song flow:

Pick your first song — recommendations → search → select. The referral song is shown first in the recommendations.

Song view (routed by content availability): official tab exists → "How do you want to play?" (chords / tabs) → song view; no official tab → we skip the chords/tabs step (an extra request is sent, the user briefly sees a loader) and show the top-rated UGC version directly.

PW2 — contextual "Choose your plan" over the song. On dismiss → PW2 closes first, then after a 300 ms delay the free top-rated UGC version (user tab) opens — without the delay the user could stay stuck on the off-tab.

Community version page: the trial step and the interstitial are removed (the interstitial on the first automatic entry to Community was already removed earlier and is replaced by our off-tab mechanic); ad blocks are kept — we do not touch ads. The version "Promo" screen is a standalone element: no ads inside it, but it keeps our off-tab promo.

C — Version chooser on dismiss. Identical to B up to PW2. Diverges only on PW2 dismiss: instead of dropping silently to UGC, the user gets a Choose your version screen — official version highlighted, all available free community versions listed below (show all free versions, not limited to 3 — the whole page scrolls; paid / interactive versions hidden: Pro tabs, Sing, etc.; keep the version category headers — chords / tabs / drums — same as on the regular tab page). From there: Start 7-day trial → feature paywall; or pick a free community version → selected community chord view.

[/macro]

Iteration 3
[macro]
true #7622   -
Exposure event: Tour Post Decline Gift Offer Close

## Decision

Conclusion

Final — trial windows closed (recalculated 2026-07-24). Money and conversion metrics are final; churn 14d / refund 14d get their final read in early August.

iOS — roll out variation B (guided first song). ARPU +20.06% (significant), driven by conversion (members → subscribers +13.11%, members → buyers +16.19%); the hypothesis (+10–20% ARPU) is met. Projected rollout impact ≈ +$1,049/day .

iOS, variation C (version chooser) — significant after maturation, but weaker than B. ARPU +11.84% is price-driven (AOV/ARPPU up, buyers flat) and C leaks non-buyers to free community versions; ≈ +$620/day. Do not prefer it over B.

Android — no effect. Nothing is significant in either variation, and the test reached only ~40% of the designed sample — underpowered, not disproven.

### Next steps

Can roll out variation B on iOS

but all product metrics retention and tab views dropped significantly so, it might not me a good option

Re-read churn 14d / refund 14d once the pending 14d charges share drops below 5% (early August); revisit the rollout only if B's churn materially degrades.

Android: relaunch on a larger sample (the arms reached ~40% of design) before accepting or rejecting the flow there.

## Forecast (per day)

Audience: users who fire Tour Post Decline Gift Offer Close (the experiment start event) and have no paid rights — the population the flow addresses. Fresh first-entry reach over the 14 complete days ending 2026-07-12: 7,699 iOS and 8,108 Android new users per day; the reconstructed filter reproduces the experiment cohort 1:1 (73,504 vs 73,503 bucketed users on iOS). The forecast below assumes a full rollout to that audience.

## Insights

B sells to more people, not at a higher price. On iOS the whole ARPU lift comes from conversion (buyers +16.19%, subscribers +13.11%), while AOV moves only +3.52% (p = 0.19) — guiding the user into the first song puts more new users in front of premium content and its paywall.

C works the other way round — and turned significant after maturation (ARPU +11.84%, p = 0.031). The version chooser lifts AOV +7.04% (p = 0.011) and ARPPU +7.54% (p = 0.006) but not the number of buyers (+4.00%, p = 0.39): it mostly steers users who were already willing to pay towards a more expensive plan instead of catching droppers.

Trial quality does not degrade. After maturation trial → charge is slightly positive in both variations on iOS (+6.34% / +2.19%, both insignificant), so the extra subscriptions are not "cheap" trials that never convert. Churn 14d in B softened to +8.52% (p = 0.24); its final read comes once the 14d charge windows close in early August.

Groups are balanced. iOS 24,685 / 24,316 / 24,503 and Android 18,760 / 18,572 / 18,605 members — under 1.5% spread, no SRM concern.

The platform gap is not only a sample-size story. Android's access funnel is half as wide (accesses per member in control: 3.40% vs 6.82% on iOS), so the same flow has much less premium surface to sell against there.

[macro]
Post tour funnel
Q: did the new first-song flow improve conversion to official tab accesses?

The tour funnel itself holds up: on iOS 95% of users who see "what to play" pick a song, 99% pass "how to play", and 77% reach the official tab — where 99% see the paywall. Android is the same shape (92% / 98% / 66%).

Inside the tour ( from_tour ) the paywall still converts poorly: iOS tab view → access is 0.43% in B. The first session sells badly, exactly as in iterations 1–2.

The money comes from the sessions after the tour ( other ): on iOS access rate is 4.64% in B vs 3.41% in control (+36%), on Android 2.32% vs 1.86% (+24%). The guided first song does not sell on the spot — it pushes users into premium content, and they buy on a later visit. This is what the ARPU lift on iOS is made of.

The version chooser (C) leaks users into free content. Of the C users who reach the official tab, most see the "Choose your version" screen (iOS 10,311 views) and 88% of them click a free community version (9,125 clicks). C's tab view → access inside the tour looks higher (2.54% vs 0.43%) only because purchases move to the "Official Tabs Choose Version" surface, while its other access rate (3.26%) falls back to control level — consistent with C lifting AOV/ARPPU but not the number of buyers.

C also produces fewer official tab views overall (iOS 11,947 vs 14,276 in B): sending non-buyers to the version list instead of the community tab costs premium impressions.

[macro]
Why v2 grew — charge-rate decomposition
Charge-rate growth decomposed against the canonical cohort and the full legit offer-viewer population. Sources: Egor sloperator results (server-side billing) plus raw-events queries 27461 / 27462.

What we measured ctrl v2 Δ (v2 vs ctrl) query

Charge rate — canonical cohort 2.97% 3.62% +22% 27462

Charge rate — ALL legit offer-viewers 3.71% 4.31% +16% 27461

├ Buy rate (share who purchased) 6.37% 7.14% +12% 27462

└ Buy→charge completion 45.6% 49.8% +9% 27462

Instant (share among buyers) 78% 75% ≈ 27462

Churn — canonical 0.10% 4.17% +4.1 p.p. 27462

Churn — ALL legit offer-viewers 1.43% 5.45% +4.0 p.p. 27461

Returned (did not buy, still active) — canonical 93.5% 88.7% −4.8 p.p. 27462

└ of those, returned within the SAME session 99.8% 98.4% ≈ 27462

Returning free user → paid later ~5 users ~5 users ≈0 27462

Tag coverage (item_id=7355 among legit offer-viewers) 97.6% 97.6% — 27461

"Untagged" extra (high-intent instant buyers, balanced) 215 users 208 users (2.4%) — 27461

Conclusion

+22% charge is genuine growth through conversion (+12% more buyers × +9% better completion to payment), not reach. On the full legit population it also grows — +16% .

Cost: v2 adds +4 p.p. of churn (left the paywall without action).

Returns are almost all within the same session; almost none of the returning free users convert to payment later.

[/macro]

[macro]
Investigation
Context. In the first session, a new user goes through onboarding, selects a song, and opens its official version (Official Tab). A paywall is shown before the content is delivered. We compared the test groups (B) of both iterations — they were designed as the same mechanic, the third one on a larger sample.

What is the same (not a difference):

Entry to the official tab: 73.3% vs 74.9%.

First paywall — on a timer ~5–6 seconds after entering the tab, in both versions, the same screen ( Official Tabs Post Decline , 62.8% vs 62.5%).

What differs (the only difference):

iter2-B: after the first paywall the user stays on the tab or leaves freely (another tab, search). There is no second paywall, the same one repeats. Sequence Post Decline → Post Decline .

iter3-B: a second, different paywall appears — Official Tabs Recently Viewed . It fires when opening a recently-viewed official tab (≈69% of impressions — on tab open, ~26% — via "back"), and the "play your first song" guided flow deliberately cycles through such tabs. Paywall dismiss → return to the tab or open the next one → paywall again. Reach 43.5% (vs 6.4%). Sequence Post Decline → Recently Viewed → Recently Viewed → … . There is no exit to Explore — after dismissing the paywall, 0.1% / ~0% go to Explore, the user is locked on the tab.

In other words, iter3-B = iter2-B plus a retention loop: a second paywall, re-triggered on every entry to a recently-viewed tab. There are no other differences.

What to look at in the data:

the Official Tabs Recently Viewed paywall — the marker of iter3-B (reach 6.4% → 43.5%);

the next event after a paywall dismiss — in iter3-B it's the tab again / the next tab (Explore ~0%), in iter2-B the user leaves freely;

paywall sequences within a session ( Post Decline → Recently Viewed ×N vs Post Decline → Post Decline ).

How we checked:

Cohorts by App Experiment Start , variation=2 (clean test group B in both iterations), first 3 days, first session, iOS+Android, bot filter.

The first pull across all groups produced false differences (tab entries +15 pp, "doubling" of Post Decline) — recomputing on clean B-vs-B showed this was contamination from the control group, and the differences collapsed.

Traced Recently Viewed further: what triggers it (tab open, not "back") and where the dismiss leads (to the tab, not to Explore).

[/macro]

[macro]
Significance analysis (Iteration 2)
### What we wanted to do

Understand what killed iteration 1 -- the mechanics (auto-playing the tab + a 15-second wait before the paywall) or the mere fact of showing the official tab to a new user.

To isolate this, we removed all the mechanics: we kept the entry into the official tab after PW1 dismiss, but showed the paywall immediately -- no playback, no timer, on a promise.

### Results

iOS: purchases through the official tab nearly doubled; retention stopped declining (in iteration 1 it dropped noticeably).

Android: conversion into the official tab also rose, but total revenue did not increase.

Total revenue: iOS +9%, but this is mostly a shift of purchases into the official tab from other places; Android -- zero, a pure shift.

### Insights

Iteration 1 was killed by the forced-playback mechanic, not the format. Removing the 15-second "let me listen before the sale" restored both conversion and retention.

The flow barely loses people along the way: 80--94% reach the tab and the paywall. The weak point is the paywall itself, not the path to it.

The uplift is largely a shift of the purchase to a more favorable moment, not new money. On Android there is no new money at all.

Sample size was roughly 3x smaller than planned, so the revenue gain is not yet proven -- we see the direction, not the amount.

### Retention

The current version does not hurt retention (iOS 7d -0.6%, 14d +4.9%, not significant). The significant drop happened in the previous version with the 15-second playback (iOS 7d -6.3%, 14d -6.4%) -- removing the mechanic removed the drop.

### Conclusions

Showing the official tab to a new user is fine -- the format works, and we build on it.

We must not let the user "get their fill" of the product before the offer: it both suppresses the purchase and hurts retention.

The flow does not yet generate money -- it shifts it to a more favorable moment; the real gain still needs to be confirmed.

[/macro]

[macro]
Significance analysis (Iteration 3)
### What we wanted to do

Relaunch the iteration-2 flow (B) on a large sample to prove the ARPU lift at significance, and try to catch the droppers with a third variation (C): after the user dismisses the paywall over the song, instead of silently dropping them to a free community version, we show a "Choose your version" screen.

Data note: the numbers below are preliminary — trials have not fully matured (pending trials 6–13% per branch, above the 5% gate) and 14-day churn/refund rest on about a quarter of matured charges.

### Results

iOS, B — the hypothesis holds. ARPU +17.3% (p = 0.002), members → buyers +13.9% (p = 0.004), members → subscribers +13.1% (p < 0.001). In the Official tabs segment ARPU is +52% (p < 0.001). The +10–20% ARPU target is met.

iOS, C — not proven. ARPU +9.5% is not significant (p = 0.09) and buyers barely move (+2.1%, p = 0.65), but AOV +6.7% (p = 0.017) and ARPPU +7.2% (p = 0.010) are.

Android — nothing. Neither variation is significant (B: ARPU −3.9%, p = 0.70; C: +9.3%, p = 0.39). The arms reached ~18.6k users each against 46,566 by design, so this is an underpowered null, not a proven zero.

The money is not made inside the tour. The flow itself barely leaks (iOS: 95% pick a song, 99% pass "how to play", 77% reach the official tab, 99% of those see the paywall), yet in-tour conversion to purchase stays at 0.43%. The lift shows up in later sessions: access rate on ordinary official-tab visits is 4.64% in B vs 3.41% in control on iOS (+36%), 2.32% vs 1.86% on Android.

### Insights

B sells more subscriptions, not more expensive ones. The whole ARPU gain comes from conversion; AOV moves only +3.1% (p = 0.25). Guiding the new user into a first song puts them in front of premium content, and they buy on a later visit.

C works the opposite way and leaks users into free content. 88% of the users who reach the "Choose your version" screen click a free community version (iOS: 9,125 clicks out of 10,311 views). It raises the value of the purchases that do happen, but not their number — and it produces fewer official-tab impressions overall (11,947 vs 14,276 in B).

We are paying for the lift with engagement. Long tab-view users drop significantly in B (iOS −5.5% to −7.3%, Android −7.5% to −8.9%, p < 0.001): the guided flow pushes users to a paywalled tab instead of content they can actually use.

On Android we pay the price without the gain — retention and engagement fall as much as on iOS, while revenue does not move at all.

### Retention

iOS, B: retention drops significantly — 1d −7.8%, 7d −4.8%, 14d −4.9% (all p < 0.001). This is new: in iteration 2 the drop had disappeared. The larger sample now resolves it clearly.

iOS, C: borderline — 7d −2.2% (p = 0.09), 14d −2.1% (p = 0.07); the version chooser hurts retention roughly half as much as B.

Android: both variations drop — 7d −5.8% and 14d −6.1% for B, −5.8% / −5.8% for C (all p < 0.001).

So the flow is not retention-neutral. The trade is explicit: more first-session revenue against a few percent of returning users.

### Conclusions

B on iOS is the only rollout candidate. It delivers the ARPU lift the project was built for (≈ +$877/day net revenue at full rollout to the eligible audience), and this time at significance.

But it is not free: −4.9% retention 14d and −6% engagement are significant, so the rollout call is a trade-off decision, not an automatic yes.

C is not confirmed — the version chooser does not catch droppers, it hands them free content. If it is kept, it should be reworked so that the free version is not the easiest exit.

Android: do not roll out. No revenue effect, retention down, and the sample never reached the design — a relaunch on the planned volume is needed before any verdict.

Final call only after trials mature and the experiment is recalculated.

[/macro]

## Next steps

Prove the revenue impact off the back of v2: conversions ↑, ARPU ↑, retention does not drop, life rate responds.

Version-selection screen (ad-hoc, the simplest option): let non-buyers choose a version instead of redirecting them to the community version.
